Cylindrical Networks and Total Nonnegativity
Robert Angarone

Abstract
. We prove that an infinite block-Toeplitz matrix with finite diagonal support is totally nonnegative if and only if it is the weight matrix of a cylindrical network. This generalizes a well-known theorem of Brenti concerning finite totally nonnegative matrices and planar networks; in particular, our work gives an alternative, self-contained proof of the non-square case. Our argument employs Temperley-Lieb immanants, first introduced by Rhoades and Skandera, which are certain elements of Lusztig’s dual canonical bases. As an application, we also obtain a new proof of a well-known theorem relating totally nonnegative block-Toeplitz matrices to interlacing polynomials.



ifvmodeempty
empty

1.
Introduction

A real matrix is called
totally nonnegative
(TN) if all of its minors, i.e. the determinants of its square submatrices, are nonnegative. A
Toeplitz
matrix is one which is constant along diagonals, i.e. entry
(i,j)
is always equal to entry
(i+1,j+1)
. Accordingly, an infinite block-Toeplitz matrx is one of this form:

\begin{bmatrix}A_{0}&A_{1}&A_{2}&A_{3}&\ldots&A_{i}&A_{i+1}&A_{i+2}\ldots\\
0&A_{0}&A_{1}&A_{2}&\ldots&A_{i-1}&A_{i}&A_{i+1}\ldots\\
0&0&A_{0}&A_{1}&\ldots&A_{i-2}&A_{i-1}&A_{i}&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}=T\left(\underline{A}\right),

where
\underline{A}=(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)
is a sequence of real matrices which all have the same dimensions. If they are all
n\times m
matrices, we may also call such a matrix
(n,m)
-periodic
, since in this case the block-Toeplitz condition is equivalent to requiring that entry
(i,j)
must always be equal to entry
(i+n,j+m)
. We say that such a matrix has
finite diagonal support
if the sequence
\underline{A}
has only finitely many nonzero terms.

A
cylindrical network
is a planar, edge-weighted, acyclic digraph embedded on a cylinder, with
n
labelled sources on one boundary component and
m
labelled sinks on the other.
We associate to each cylindrical network
N
an infinite block-Toeplitz matrix called its
weight matrix

W(N)
. For
i\leq n,\,j\leq m,
and
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, the
(i+wn,\,j+w^{\prime}m)
entry of
W(N)
is given by
\sum_{p}\mathrm{wt}(p)
, where the sum ranges over all directed paths
p
in
N
from source
i
to sink
j
that wrap around the cylinder
w^{\prime}-w
times, and
\mathrm{wt}(p)
is the product of all the edges in
p
. The primary contribution of this paper is the following result:

Theorem 1.1
.

Any infinite,
(n,m)
-periodic TN block-Toeplitz matrix with finite diagonal support is equal to the weight matrix of some cylindrical network with
n
sources,
m
sinks, and nonnegative edge weights.

When considered in tandem with results of Lam and Pylyavskyy, our main theorem completes a cylindrical, infinite generalization of a well-known story concerning
planar
networks and
finite
TN matrices. Brenti proved that any finite TN matrix is equal to the weight matrix of a finite planar network with nonnegative edge weights
[
brenti-tp
]
. Conversely, the famed Gessel-Lindström-Viennot lemma implies that the weight matrix of any finite planar network with nonnegative edge weights is TN
[
lindstrom
,
gessel-viennot
]
.

Our Theorem
1.1
is the cylindrical generalization of Brenti’s theorem, while Lam and Pylyavskyy introduced the cylindrical generalziation of the Gessel-Lindström-Viennot lemma
[
lam-pylyavskyy
]
. Lam and Pylyavskyy phrase their results in terms of the
totally nonnegative loop group
, a construction equivalent to invertible
(n,n)
-periodic matrices.

In Sections
2.1
through
2.3
, we place the main theorem in context and recall some important known results. In Sections
2.4
and
2.5
, we recall key lemmas concerning Temperley-Lieb immanants, which are used to great extent in the proof of the main theorem. Section
3
is dedicated to proving the result; in particular, Section
3.1
explains how the main result reduces to Proposition
3.7
, and Section
3.3
is dedicated to proving that proposition. In Section
, we discuss applications of our technique to interlacing polynomials and pose an open question in this vein.

2.
Background

2.1.
Totally Nonnegative Matrices

Although TN matrices were first studied in analysis, they have since become of great interest to combinatorialists. This is due to their appearance in the theory of real-rooted and log-concave polynomials
[
brenti-polya
]
, their prominent role in laying the groundwork for the theory of cluster algebras
[
FZ-tests-params
,
FZ-clusters-1
]
, and more. We refer the reader to
[
brenti-tp
,
FZ-tests-params
]
for more comprehensive overviews of total positivity.

If
I
is a subset of the rows of
M
and
J
is a subset of its columns, we let
M[I,J]
denote the submatrix of
M
with row set
I
and column set
J
. We also allow for
I
and
J
to be multisets of the rows and columns of
M
; in this way, we form
generalized
submatrices of
M
. Note that if
M
is a TN matrix, then any generalized submatrix of
M
is also TN.

The following theorem, known as the Loewner-Whitney theorem, gives simple generators for the set of invertible TN matrices.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
Loewner
,
whitney
]
)
.

As a semigroup, the set of invertible TN matrices is generated by matrices of the following forms, for nonnegative
a
,
b
, and
c
:

\begin{bmatrix}1\\
&1\\
&&\ddots\\
&&&a\\
&&&&\ddots\\
&&&&&1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}1\\
&1\\
&&\ddots\\
&&&1&b\\
&&&&\ddots\\
&&&&&1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}1\\
&1\\
&&\ddots\\
&&&1\\
&&&c&\ddots\\
&&&&&1\end{bmatrix}.

2.2.
The Gessel-Lindström-Viennot Lemma

The following fundamental result, due to Lindström and later Gessel-Viennot, establishes a combinatorial method for generating TN matrices. It is a cornerstone of algebraic combinatorics, and is used, for example, to provide a modern proof of the Jacobi-Trudi identity.

Definition 2.2
.

Suppose
N
is a finite, planar, acyclic, edge-weighted digraph with
n
sources and
m
sinks (i.e. a
planar network
). Then the weight matrix
W(N)
is given by

W(N)_{i,j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{paths }p\\
\text{ source }i\to\text{ sink }j\end{subarray}}\mathrm{wt}(p),\qquad\mathrm{wt}(p):=\prod_{e\in p}\mathrm{wt}(e).

Lemma 2.3

(
[
lindstrom
,
gessel-viennot
]
)
.

Suppose
N
is a planar network as in Definition
2.2
. Given subsets
I=\{i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
and
J=\{j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{k}\}\subseteq[m]
, we have

\left|W(N)[I,J]\right|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{noncrossing path families }\\
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\\
p_{\ell}:\text{ source }i_{\ell}\to\text{ sink }j_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathrm{wt}(p_{k}).

In particular, if
N
has nonnegative edge weights, then
W(N)
is totally nonnegative.

What is especially surprising is that the converse of Lemma
2.3
is true; that is, given any totally nonnegative matrix
M
, there exists a planar network
N
with nonnegative edge weights such that
W(N)=M
. If
M
is invertible, this can be proven by showing that each of the matrices in Theorem
2.1
can be represented as a network. One then observes that concatenating networks, sink-to-source, corresponds to matrix multiplicaiton. If
M
is not invertible, or perhaps not even square, it can still be represented by a network, but one must use a more complicated argument. This was first observed by Brenti
[
brenti-tp
]
.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
brenti-tp
]
)
.

Any TN matrix is the weight matrix of a planar network with nonnegative edge weights.

Brenti’s argument relies on a certain tridiagonal factorization theorem, ultimately coming from theorems in
[
ando
]
and
[
cryer
]
. Our proof of the cylindrical version of this theorem will also involve a factorization result; Brenti’s methods, however, do not immediately apply to our case. While we could apply existing results involving finite TN matrices locally on any square submatrix of a given infinite TN matrix, the difficulty is in showing that we can find a network (equivalently, factorization) structure which is compatible globally with the entire matrix.

2.3.
Total Nonnegativity in the Loop Group

Lam and Pylyavskyy
[
lam-pylyavskyy
]
introduced an extension of the notion of total nonnegativity to matrices whose entries are polynomials (or power series) with real coefficients, rather than real numbers. These turn out to be a special case of
(n,m)
-periodic matrices called the
loop group
. In this section, we recall their core results concerning the relationship between the totally nonnegative loop group and cylindrical networks.

Definition 2.5
.

The
n\times n
polynomial loop group
is the set of
n\times n
matrices with Laurent polynomial entries whose determinants are nonzero monomials. An element of the loop group is called a
loop
.

Definition 2.6
.

Suppose
M
is an
n\times m
matrix with formal Laurent series entries. Let
M_{d}
be the matrix whose
i,j
th entry is the coefficient of
t^{d}
in
M_{i,j}
. Then define the
unfolding
of
M
to be the infinite block-Toeplitz matrix with copies of
M_{i}
along the
i
th block-diagonal. We say
M
is TN if its unfolding is TN.

Definition 2.7
.

Define the
folding
of an infinite
(n,m)
-periodic matrix
M
to be the unique
n\times m
matrix with Laurent series entries whose unfolding is
M
.

Remark 2.8
.

Unfolding and folding preserve multiplication and addition of matrices.

Example 2.9
.

Here is a member of the
2\times 2
loop group and its associated unfolding:

\displaystyle{\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\dfrac{1}{1-6t}&\dfrac{2}{1-6t}\\
\dfrac{3t}{1-6t}&\dfrac{1}{1-6t}\\
\end{array}\right]}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}6&12\\
3&6\end{pmatrix}t+\begin{pmatrix}36&72\\
18&36\end{pmatrix}t^{2}+\ldots

\displaystyle\underset{\text{Unfold}}{\leadsto}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc|cc|c}1&2&6&12&36&72&\ldots\\
0&1&3&6&18&36&\ldots\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1&2&6&12&\ldots\\
0&0&0&1&3&6&\ldots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{array}\right]

Definition 2.10
.

A
cylindrical network
is finite, planar, edge-weighted, acyclic digraph embedded on a cylinder, with
n
sources
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}
on one boundary component and
m
sinks
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{m}
on the other, along with a distinguished line
\mathfrak{h}
connecting the boundary components of the cylinder. By default,
\mathfrak{h}
originates between sources
v_{1}
and
v_{n}
, terminates between sinks
w_{1}
and
w_{m}
, and does not intersect any vertex of
N
.

Definition 2.11
.

Given a path
p
in a cylindrical network
N
with line
\mathfrak{h}
, define
\mathrm{rot}(p)
to be the
rotor
of
p
: the number of times
p
crosses
\mathfrak{h}
counterclockwise minus the number of times
p
crosses
\mathfrak{h}
clockwise.

Definition 2.12
.

Given
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\overline{i}\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
be equal to
i\bmod n
; similarly for
\overline{j}
and
m
. Then a path
p
in a cylindrical network
N
is said to be an
(i,j)
-path
if it begins at source
v_{\overline{i}}
, ends at sink
w_{\overline{j}}
, and has
\mathrm{rot}(p)=\left(\left(j-\overline{j}\right)/m\right)-\left(\left(i-\overline{i}\right)/n\right)
.

Remark 2.13
.

We will always draw cylindrical networks with sources on the left, sinks on the right, and the line
\mathfrak{h}
on top. A crossing of
\mathfrak{h}
is ‘counterclockwise’ if it would be counterclockwise when viewed from the right of the cylinder; in other words, it is counterclockwise if it moves from the back of our view in the diagrams to the front; see Example
2.16

Intuitively, when forming an
(i,j)
-path, one should think that crossing the line
\mathfrak{h}
counterclockwise has the effect of increasing all sink labels by
m
, as though we crossed into a new copy of the cylinder. Accordingly, crossing
\mathfrak{h}
clockwise has the effect of decreasing path labels by
m
.

Definition 2.14
.

Given a cylindrical network
N
, the
folded weight matrix
of
N
is the
n\times m
matrix given by

\overline{W}(N)_{i,j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{paths }p\\
v_{i}\to w_{j}\end{subarray}}t^{\mathrm{rot}(p)}\mathrm{wt}(p).

The
unfolded weight matrix
is the infinite matrix given by

W(N)_{i,j}=\sum_{(i,j)-\text{paths }p}\mathrm{wt}(p).

Note that the unfolded weight matrix is
(n,m)
-periodic, and indeed is the unfolding of the folded weight matrix.

Definition 2.15
.

Suppose
p
is an
(i,j)
-path and
q
is an
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
-path in a cylindrical network
N
such that the two paths intersect at vertex
c
. Then let
\tilde{p}
and
\tilde{q}
be the paths obtained by following either
p
or
q
until the point
c
, then following the other path afterwards. We say that
p
and
q
are
properly crossing
if
\tilde{p}
is an
(i,j^{\prime})
-path (equivalently, if
\tilde{q}
is an
(i,j^{\prime})
-path); the paths are called
uncrossed
otherwise.

Example 2.16
.

Below is a cylindrical network with three source vertices, three sink vertices, and one additional vertex. All edges are assumed to have weight
1
. The line
\mathfrak{h}
coincides with the top of the picture. Dotted portions of edges are assumed to lie on the back side of the cylinder. The red path
p
from
v_{1}
to
w_{3}
is a
(1,6)
-path. The green path
r
from
v_{2}
to
w_{2}
is a
(2,2)
-path. The blue path
q
from
v_{3}
to
w_{1}
is a
(3,4)
-path. The paths
p
and
q
are uncrossed, even though they intersect.

The folded and unfolded weight matrices for this network are as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&0&t^{-1}\\
0&1&0\\
t&0&1\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{Unfold}}{\leadsto}\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc|ccc|c}1&0&0&0&0&0&\ldots\\
0&1&0&0&0&0&\ldots\\
0&0&1&1&0&0&\ldots\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1&1&0&0&\ldots\\
0&0&0&0&1&0&\ldots\\
0&0&0&0&0&1&\ldots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{array}\right]

Lam and Pylyavskyy showed that the above definitions give rise to a cylindrical analogue of the classical Gessel-Lindström-Viennot lemma.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
lam-pylyavskyy
]
)
.

Suppose
N
is a cylindrical network, and
W(N)
its weight matrix. For
I=\{i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
J=\{j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, let
\Phi(I,J)
be the set of all families of pairwise uncrossed paths
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
such that
p_{\ell}
is an
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
-path for all
1\leq\ell\leq k
. Then

|W(N)[I,J]|=\sum_{P\in\Phi(I,J)}\prod_{p\in P}\mathrm{wt}(p).

In particular, if
N
has nonnegative edge weights, then
W(N)
is totally nonnegative.

In fact, by proving factorization theorems for the loop group, Lam and Pylyavskyy were also able to prove the following partial generalization of Brenti’s theorem.

Theorem 2.18

(
[
lam-pylyavskyy
]
)
.

Any
(n,n)
-periodic, invertible block-Toeplitz TN matrix is the weight matrix of a cylindrical network with nonnegative edge weights.

Our primary goal in the remainder of the paper is to extend this generalization to include
(n,m)
-periodic matrices for which
n\neq m
and which are not necessarily invertible. The proofs methods of Lam and Pylyavskyy will not readily extend to this context. Although we will also proceed by factorization argument, those authors relied on a lemma which applies only to full-rank matrices—namely that, for such matrices, checking that only row- or column-solid minors are positive is enough for total positivity. Instead, we will employ an argument which checks all minors explicitly, and which employs certain combinatorial matrix functions which we describe next.

2.4.
Temperley-Lieb Immanants

In this section, we recall some facts about Temperley-Lieb immanants, first introduced by Rhoades and Skandera
[
rhoades-skandera
]
. Temperley-Lieb immanants are certain functions on matrices that are defined via the combinatorics of noncrossing matchings. They are generally unwieldy to compute. Luckily, we need not compute even a single Temperley-Lieb immanant explicitly in order to prove the main theorem. Instead, we just rely on a pair of key results from Rhoades and Skandera, which appear in this work as Lemmas
2.27
and
2.33
. The next two sections are dedicated to stating these lemmas.

Definition 2.19
.

Given
n\geq 2
and a formal parameter
\xi
, the
Temperley-Lieb algebra

\mathrm{TL}_{n}(\xi)
is a
\mathbb{C}[\xi]
-algebra generated by
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n-1}
and subject to the following relations:

t_{i}^{2}=\xi t_{i},\quad t_{i}t_{j}t_{i}=t_{i}\text{ if }|i-j|=1,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,t_{i}t_{j}=t_{j}t_{i}\text{ if }|i-j|\geq 2.

Remark 2.20
.

The multiplication rules in
\mathrm{TL}_{n}(\xi)
can be interpreted visually. To each
t_{i}
, associate a noncrossing matching of
2n
points in the following way:
\vdots
,
t_{1}
\vdots
,\ldots,
t_{2}
\vdots
.
t_{n-1}

To multiply matchings, first concatenate them left-to-right; second, remove all loops to obtain a single noncrossing matching of
2n
points; and finally, multiply the resulting diagram by a factor of
\xi
for each loop removed.

Example 2.21
.

In
\mathrm{TL}_{5}(\xi)
, we have

\displaystyle(t_{1}t_{2})(t_{2}+t_{3})

\displaystyle=t_{1}t_{2}^{2}+t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}

\displaystyle=\hbox to73.45pt{\vbox to66.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.71387pt\lower-26.05344pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

As is more apparent from the visual interpretation of multiplication, the set of
all
possible noncrossing matchings of
2n
points forms a basis for
\mathrm{TL}_{n}(\xi)
. Thus, as a
\mathbb{C}[\xi]
-vector space, the dimension of
\mathrm{TL}_{n}(\xi)
is the
n
th Catalan number
C_{n}
. For example, since
C_{3}=5
, we know
\mathrm{TL}_{3}(\xi)
is
5
-dimensional and has the following basis:

\mathcal{B}_{3}=\left\{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to30.77pt{\vbox to37.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.71387pt\lower-33.16663pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Definition 2.22
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{n}
be the set of noncrossing matchings on
2n
points, regarded as a basis for
\mathrm{TL}_{n}(\xi)
.

Definition 2.23
.

Given
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, let
\varphi_{T}:S_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be given as follows: if
w\in S_{n}
has reduced word
s_{j_{1}}s_{j_{2}}\cdots s_{j_{k}}
, let
\varphi_{T}(w)
be the coefficient of
T
when
\left(t_{j_{1}}-1\right)\left(t_{j_{2}}-1\right)\cdots\left(t_{j_{k}}-1\right)
is expanded in the Temperley-Lieb algebra
\mathrm{TL}_{n}(2)
.

Definition 2.24
.

An
immanant
is any function
I:\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
of the form

I(M)=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}\varphi(w)M_{1,w(1)}M_{2,w(2)}\cdots M_{n,w(n)}

for some function
\varphi:S_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group.

Definition 2.25
.

Given a noncrossing matching
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the
Temperley-Lieb immanant
for
T
is the function
\mathrm{imm}_{T}\colon\mathrm{Mat}_{n}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M):=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}\varphi_{T}(w)M_{1,w(1)}M_{1,w(2)}\cdots M_{n,w(n)}.

Remark 2.26
.

When the matrix
M
is understood, we will frequently draw the noncrossing matching
T
rather than write
\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M)
.

Lemma 2.27

(Rhoades-Skandera
[
rhoades-skandera
]
)
.

Suppose
M
is a totally nonnegative
n\times n
matrix. Then for any
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we have
\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M)\geq 0
.

The positivity property in Lemma
2.27
is remarkable. It remains an intriguing open problem to classify all functions which are nonnegative on totally nonnegative matrices; these are known as
totally positive functions
. It is known, for example, that immanants obtained by setting
\varphi
equal to an irreducible
S_{n}
character are totally positive
[
stembridge
]
.

2.5.
Complementary Minor Immanants and Their Diagrams

In this section, we describe notation for complementary minor immanants, i.e. pairs of minors with complementary row and column sets. This notation was introduced by Rhoades and Skandera
[
rhoades-skandera
]
to state Lemma
2.33
, which will be indispensable in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Definition 2.28
.

Given an
n\times n
matrix
M
and
I,J\subseteq[n]
with
|I|=|J|
, the
complementary minor immanant
of
M
with respect to
I
and
J
is

\mathcal{C}_{I,J}(M):=\left|M[I,J]\right|\cdot\left|M[[n]\setminus I,\,\,[n]\setminus J]\right|.

Definition 2.29
.

Given
I,J\subseteq[n]
with
|I|=|J|
, the associated
complementary minor diagram
is a
2\times n
array of dots colored black or white in the following way:

•

in the left column, color the
j
th dot from the top black if
j\in J
and white otherwise;

•

in the right column, color the
i
th dot from the top white if
i\in I
and black otherwise.

Example 2.30
.

If
n=5
,
I=\{1,2,4\}
, and
J=\{1,3,5\}
, Definition
2.29
gives the following diagram:

Remark 2.31
.

Since
I
and
J
can be read off of a complementary minor diagram, when the matrix
M
is understood, we will frequently draw a complementary minor diagram rather than writing
\mathcal{C}_{I,J}(M)
.

Definition 2.32
.

Given
I,J\subseteq[n]
with
|I|=|J|
, let
\Theta(I,J)
be the set of noncrossing matchings
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
such that every strand in
T
connects a white dot to a black dot in the complementary minor diagram associated to
I
and
J
. If
T\in\Theta(I,J)
, we say
T
is
(I,J)
-compatible.

Lemma 2.33

(Rhoades-Skandera
[
rhoades-skandera
]
)
.

For any
n\times n
matrix
M
and
I,J\subseteq[n]
with
|I|=|J|
, we have

\mathcal{C}_{I,J}(M)=\sum_{T\in\Theta(I,J)}\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M).

.

2.33
to the complementary minor diagram from Example
2.30

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We are suppressing the matrix
M
in both sides of the equation; see Remarks
2.26
and
2.31
.

Corollary 2.35
.

If
M
is a totally nonnegative matrix and
T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
is
(I,J)
-compatible for some pair of sets
I,J
such that
\mathcal{C}_{I,J}(M)=0,
then
\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M)=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.33
, the immanant
\mathcal{C}_{I,J}
is a positive linear combination of Temperley-Lieb immanants, one of which is
\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M)
. By Lemma
2.27
, each of those summands is nonnegative. Since their sum is
\mathcal{C}_{I,J}(M)=0
, each Temperley-Lieb immanant in that sum must be
0
individually. In particular,
\mathrm{imm}_{T}(M)=0
.
∎

3.
Main Result

3.1.
Southwest Corner Elimination

In this section, we explain how Theorem
1.1
reduces to Proposition
3.7
, which states that certain row operations on infinite block-Toeplitz matrices preserve total nonnegativity. The main idea is that any given
(n,m)
-periodic TN matrix
M
can be written as
M=R\cdot M^{\prime}
, where
R
is a simple matrix achieving a periodic row operation and
M^{\prime}
is an
(n,m)
-periodic matrix with fewer nonzero entries. The matrix
R
will easily be seen to be representable as a cylindrical network; that
M^{\prime}
is representable will follow by induction. Before presenting Proposition
3.7
, we first provide some prerequisite definitions. Then we will prove Theorem
1.1
on the assumption of Proposition
3.7
.

Definition 3.1
.

We say that an
(n,m)
-periodic matrix
M^{\prime}
is obtained from another such matrix
M
via a
periodic elementary row operation
if

M^{\prime}_{i,j}=\begin{cases}M_{i,j}-c\cdot M_{i-1,j}&\text{ if }i\equiv k\bmod n\\
M_{i,j}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

For some real constant
c
and residue class
k
modulo
n
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
M=R\cdot M^{\prime}
, where
M^{\prime}
is obtained from
M
via a periodic elementary row operation. Then the matrix
R
is equal to the weight matrix of a cylindrical network.

Proof.

Observe that
R
is an
(n,n)
-periodic matrix with
1
s on the main diagonal, the constant
c
in all entries of the form
(k,k-1)
modulo
n
, and
0
s elsewhere. This is the weight matrix of the cylindrical network with
n
edges of weight
1
from each source
v_{i}
to the corresponding sink
w_{i}
, and one edge of weight
c
from source
v_{k}
to sink
w_{k-1}
(taking indices modulo
n
). For example, with
n=7
and
k=3
, we obtain the following network:

\includegraphics[scale]{Tikz-Pictures/elem_row_network_1.png/elem_row_network_1.png.001.png}.\qed

Definition 3.3
.

A matrix
M
is
row-convex
if there does not exist a row index
\ell
and column indices
i<j<k
so that
M_{\ell,i}\neq 0
,
M_{\ell,j}=0
, and
M_{\ell,k}\neq 0
; in other words, each row of the matrix is a sequence with no internal zeroes. Define
column-convex
matrices similarly, and say a matrix has
convex support
if it is both row- and column-convex.

Proposition 3.4
.

Any TN matrix with no row or column of only zeroes has convex support.

Proof.

Suppose
M
is such a matrix and suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that it does not have convex support. Then it is either not row-convex or not column-convex. In the former case, we may find a row index
\ell
and column indices
i<j<k
so that
M_{\ell,i}\neq 0
,
M_{\ell,j}=0
, and
M_{\ell,k}\neq 0
. Since the matrix has no columns of all zeroes, there exists a nonzero entry in column
j
. If
M_{\ell^{\prime},j}\neq 0
for
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, then the following minor of
M
is negative, a contradiction:

\left|\begin{matrix}M_{\ell^{\prime},i}&M_{\ell^{\prime},j}\\
M_{\ell,i}&M_{\ell,j}\end{matrix}\right|=M_{\ell^{\prime},i}M_{\ell,j}-M_{\ell^{\prime},j}M_{\ell,i}=M_{\ell^{\prime},i}\cdot 0-M_{\ell^{\prime},j}M_{\ell,i}=-M_{\ell^{\prime},j}M_{\ell,i}.

Similarly, if
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
, we find the following negative minor:

\left|\begin{matrix}M_{\ell,j}&M_{\ell,k}\\
M_{\ell^{\prime},j}&M_{\ell^{\prime},k}\end{matrix}\right|=-M_{\ell,k}M_{\ell^{\prime},j}.

A similar argument can be used to show column-convexity.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

A
SW corner
of a matrix is a nonzero entry with all entries below and to the left of it equal to
0
. A
special SW corner
is a SW corner
M_{i,j}
which is also the only nonzero entry below or to the left of
M_{i-1,j}
. Visually, a special SW corner looks like

\star

where
\star
represents the special SW corner itself. Entries in solid blue are required to be nonzero, entries in gray are required to be
0
, and entries in light blue could be zero or nonzero.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
M
is a TN
(n,m)
-periodic infinite matrix with finite diagonal support, no rows of all zeroes, and
n>m
. Then
M
has a special SW corner.

Proof.

Let
\ell(i)=\min\{j:M_{i,j}\neq 0\}
be the column index of the leftmost nonzero entry in row
i
; this is well-defined since we assumed
M
has finite diagonal support. By Proposition
3.1
,
M
is column-convex, so the sequence
\ell(1),\ell(2),\ldots
is either weakly increasing or decreasing. Since
M
is
(n,m)
-periodic, we have
\ell(i+n)=\ell(i)+m
, so the sequence must in fact be weakly increasing.

Observe that
\ell(1),\ell(2),\ldots,\ell(n+1)
is a weakly increasing sequence with
\ell(n+1)-\ell(1)=m
. In other words, it is an increasing sequence of length
n+1
that takes at most
m+1<n+1
values. Thus two consecutive elements in this sequence must be equal; suppose
\ell(i^{\star}-1)
and
\ell(i^{\star})
are the last such pair and let
j^{\star}=\ell(i^{\star})
.

We claim that
M_{i^{\star},j^{\star}}
is a special SW corner of
M
. By construction, it is the leftmost nonzero entry in its row, and the same is true for
M_{i^{\star}-1,j^{\star}}
. Also by construction,
M_{i^{\star}+1,j^{\star}}=0
; if this entry were nonzero, we would have
\ell(i^{\star}+1)\leq\ell(i^{\star})
, which implies
\ell(i^{\star}+1)=\ell(i^{\star})
since this sequence is weakly increasing. But we selected
i^{\star}
to be the last possible choice of
i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
with
\ell(i-1)=\ell(i)
, so this is a contradiction. Given that
M_{i^{\star}+1,j^{\star}}=0
, all other entries below or to the left of
M_{i^{\star},j^{\star}}
are
0
as well, since
M
has convex support. Thus
M_{i^{\star},j^{\star}}
satisfies all the conditions to be a special southwest corner of
M
.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose
M
is a TN
(n,m)
-periodic infinite matrix with finite diagonal support and a special SW corner. Then the matrix
M^{\prime}
obtained from
M
by eliminating a special SW corner of
M
via a periodic elementary row operation is also TN.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
, assumsing Proposition
3.7
.

Let
M
be an arbitrary TN infinite
(n,m)
-periodic matrix with finite diagonal support. We show, via an argument by cases, that we can always write
M=R\cdot M^{\prime}
or
M=M^{\prime}\cdot R
so that:

(1)

R
and
M^{\prime}
are totally nonnegative,

(2)

the matrix
R
is representable by a cylindrical network
N_{R}
, and

(3)

the matrix
M^{\prime}
has strictly fewer nonzero entries than
M
, or is
(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})
-periodic for
n^{\prime}<n
or
m^{\prime}<m
.

By induction,
M^{\prime}
is also representable by a cylindrical network
N_{M^{\prime}}
. It follows that
M
is representable by a network obtained via concatenating
N_{R}
and
N_{M^{\prime}}
. The base case is an
(\ell,\ell)
-periodic matrix supported on a single diagonal. The latter is clearly representable by a cylindrical network
N
with
\ell
sources,
\ell
sinks, and
\ell
edges.

Case I:
Suppose the matrix
M
has rows or columns of all zeroes. Suppose, in particular, that the
k
th row of
M
is all zeroes. Let
\mathrm{id}_{k}
be the
(n-1,n)
-periodic matrix which is equal to the
(n,n)
-periodic identity matrix with all rows with indices equal to
k\bmod n
removed. Then observe that
M=\mathrm{id}_{k}\cdot M^{\prime}
, where
M^{\prime}
is equal to
M
, but with every row with index equal to
k\bmod n
removed. In particular,
M^{\prime}
is
(n-1,m)
-periodic and TN. Since
M
is periodic, we only need to apply this process finitely many times before we obtain a matrix with no rows of all zeroes. Now observe that
\mathrm{id}_{k}
is equal to the weight matrix of the cylindrical network with
n-1
sources
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k-1},v_{k+1},\ldots,v_{n}
, with
n
sinks
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n}
, and with each source
v_{i}
incident to a single edge of weight
1
terminating at
w_{i}
for all
i\neq k
.

If
M
has columns of all zeroes, we apply a similar argument. When column
k
of
M
is all zeroes, we may write
M=M^{\prime}\cdot(\mathrm{id}_{k})^{\top}
, and
M^{\prime}
is equal to
M
with the
k
th column removed. Similarly to before,
M^{\prime}
is
(n,n-1)
-periodic and TN. The matrix
(\mathrm{id}_{k})^{\top}
is representable by a cylindrical network. This is because, given a network
N
, one may reverse the orientations of all edges and exchange the roles of sources and sinks to obtain a network
N^{\top}
satisfying
W(N^{\top})=\left(W(N)\right)^{\top}
.

Case II:
Suppose that
n=m
and
M
has no rows or columns of all zeroes. Then if
M
does have a special SW corner, Proposition
3.7
implies
M=R\cdot M^{\prime}
, where
R
eliminates the special SW corner of
M
via periodic elementary row operations and
M^{\prime}
is still TN. The matrix
M^{\prime}
has strictly fewer nonzero entries, and
R
is representable by Lemma
3.2
.

On the other hand, if
M
does not have a special SW corner, it is upper triangular, and therefore invertible. Using this full-rank assumption, Theorem 2.6 of
[
lam-pylyavskyy
]
allows us to eliminate a (non-special) SW corner of
M
via periodic elementary row operations, and then follow reasoning similar to the special corner case.

Case III:
Suppose
n\neq m
and
M
has no rows or columns of all zeroes. Then if
n>m
, we may apply Lemma
3.6
to find a special SW corner in
M
and eliminate it with a periodic elementary row operation via Proposition
3.7
, then proceed similarly to Case II.

If
n<m
, we apply Lemmas
3.6
and Proposition
3.7
instead to
M^{\top}
, obtaining a factorization
M^{\top}=R\cdot M^{\prime}
. Thus
M=(M^{\prime})^{\top}\cdot R^{\top}
. Following the reasoning in Case I, the matrices
(M^{\prime})^{\top}
and
R^{\top}
are representable by networks, since
M^{\prime}
and
R
themselves are.
∎

3.2.
An Example of Proposition
3.7

Proving Proposition
3.7
is the primary requirement for proving Theorem
1.1
. The proof will require several new definitions. As such, we dedicate this section to computing an example first, to motivate these definitions. The full proof is contained in the following section.

Suppose
M
is a
(2,1)
-periodic TN matrix:

M=\begin{bmatrix}b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}&\ldots&b_{n}&0&0&\ldots\\
a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{4}&\ldots&a_{n}&a_{n+1}&0&\ldots\\
0&b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&\ldots&b_{n-1}&b_{n}&0&\ldots\\
0&a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&\ldots&a_{n-1}&a_{n}&0&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}.

Then we obtain the following matrix
M^{\prime}
by eliminating a special SW corner of
M
:

M^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&\ldots&b_{n-1}&b_{n}&0&\ldots\\
a_{0}-cb_{0}&a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&\ldots&a_{n-1}-cb_{n-1}&a_{n}-cb_{n}&a_{n+1}&\ldots\\
0&b_{0}&b_{1}&\ldots&b_{n-2}&b_{n-1}&b_{n}&\ldots\\
0&a_{0}-cb_{0}&a_{1}-cb_{1}&\ldots&a_{n-2}-cb_{n-2}&a_{n-1}-cb_{n-1}&a_{n}-cb_{n}&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix},

where
c=a_{0}/b_{0}
. Notice that we have chosen to display
a_{0}-cb_{0}
in
M^{\prime}
, even though it is equal to
0
. The utility of this will be made clear later.

When proving Proposition
3.7
, we must show every submatrix of
M^{\prime}
has a nonnegative determinant. Consider the following submatrix of
M^{\prime}
:

\Delta=\begin{bmatrix}a_{4}-cb_{4}&a_{5}-cb_{5}&a_{6}-cb_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

By the multilinearity of the determinant, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Delta\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}-cb_{4}&a_{5}-cb_{5}&a_{6}-cb_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}\end{matrix}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-c\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}\end{matrix}\right|

(1)

\displaystyle=\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-c\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-c\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|+c^{2}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|.

Recalling that
c=a_{0}/b_{0}
, we may re-write the above as

(2)

\displaystyle\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-\frac{a_{0}}{b_{0}}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-\frac{a_{0}}{b_{0}}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|+\frac{a_{0}^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|.

Let us clear denominators in Expression
2
, multiplying by
b_{0}^{2}>0
to obtain

(3)

\displaystyle b_{0}^{2}\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-b_{0}a_{0}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|-b_{0}a_{0}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|+a_{0}^{2}\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\end{matrix}\right|.

Now, consider the following submatrix of
M
:

\tilde{\Delta}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{0}&b_{3}&b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
a_{0}&a_{3}&a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
0&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
0&b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\\
0&a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
M
is totally nonnegative, so is
\tilde{\Delta}
. Observe that Expression
3
is equal to

(4)

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{\Delta}[14,12]\right|\cdot\left|\tilde{\Delta}[235,345]\right|-

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{\Delta}[15,12]\right|\cdot\left|\tilde{\Delta}[234,345]\right|

\displaystyle-\left|\tilde{\Delta}[24,12]\right|\cdot\left|\tilde{\Delta}[135,345]\right|+\left|\tilde{\Delta}[25,12]\right|\cdot\left|\tilde{\Delta}[134,345]\right|.

In other words, each of the summands in Expression
3
is (up to a sign) a complementary minor immanant of
\tilde{\Delta}
. Via Definition
2.29
, we re-write Expression
4
as

(5)

\displaystyle\hbox to30.77pt{\vbox to66.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.71387pt\lower-31.74391pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Our goal is to show that this sum is nonnegative. By Theorem
2.27
, each Temperley-Lieb immanant is nonnegative individually, but two of them appear here with a minus sign. Notice, however, that the middle term is also
(34,12)
-compatible, and

\tilde{\Delta}[34,12]\cdot\tilde{\Delta}[125,345]=\left|\begin{matrix}0&b_{1}\\
0&b_{0}\end{matrix}\right|\cdot\left|\begin{matrix}b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\end{matrix}\right|=0.

Thus, by Corollary
2.35
, the middle Temperley-Lieb immanant in Expression
6
is
0
. A similar argument shows that the third term in Expression
6
is also
0
. The only remaining Temperley-Lieb immanant has a positive coefficient, so
b_{0}^{2}|\Delta|\geq 0
, and therefore
\left|\Delta\right|\geq 0
.

3.3.
The Proof of Proposition
3.7

Our goal in this section is to describe general versions of the calculations found in the previous section. For the remainder of this section, we let
M
be some fixed TN
(n,m)
-periodic infinite matrix and let
M^{\prime}
be obtained from
M
by eliminating a special SW corner at entry
(i_{\star},j_{\star})
. We also fix
a_{0}=M_{i^{\star},j^{\star}}
and
b_{0}=M_{i^{\star}-1,j^{\star}}
, so that
c=a_{0}/b_{0}
is the constant used in the periodic elementary row operation achieving this elimination. Label the nonzero entries in row
i^{\star}
as
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
and label the nonzero entries in row
i^{\star}-1
as
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
.

Given a submatrix
\Delta
of
M^{\prime}
, we build a generalized submatrix
\tilde{\Delta}
of
M
. We then show that
|\Delta|
is a nonnegative sum of Temperley-Lieb immanants of
\tilde{\Delta}
. Since
M
is assumed to be TN, we know that
\tilde{\Delta}
is TN as well, so its Temperley-Lieb immanants are nonnegative. The matrix
\tilde{\Delta}
is built specifically to reflect the expansion of
|\Delta|
via multilinearity, as in Expressions
1
and
4
.

Definition 3.8
.

For a set
X\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, let
X^{-}=\{x-1:x\in X\}
.

Definition 3.9
.

Given a submatrix
\Delta=M^{\prime}\left[I,J\right]
, let

I^{\star}=I\cap(\mathbb{N}i^{\star}),\quad A(\Delta)=\{r\in I^{\star}:r-1\not\in I\},\quad C(\Delta)=\left\{j^{\star}+km:i^{\star}+kn\in A(\Delta)\right\}.

Then define

\tilde{\Delta}=M[I\cup A(\Delta)^{-},J\sqcup C(\Delta)],

where
\sqcup
is a disjoint union. Thus, in general,
J\sqcup C(\Delta)
may contain duplicate elements, and as such,
\tilde{\Delta}
is a generalized subamtrix of
M
.

Intuitively,
A(\Delta)
is the set of rows of
\Delta
which need to be ‘dealt with’ when proving that
\Delta
is TN. If
r\not\in I^{\star}
, then
r
is already equal to a row of
M
. Additionally, if
r\in I^{\star}
and
r-1\in I
, then we can undo the row operation within
\Delta
while preserving
|\Delta|
.
The purpose of the columns
C(\Delta)
is to provide additional copies of
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
in the matrix
\tilde{\Delta}
. These will be used to represent clearing denominators after expanding
|\Delta|
via multilinearity; see Expressions
1
and
3
.

Example 3.10
.

Suppose
M
is a
(4,2)
-periodic TN matrix with a special SW corner at
(4,1)
. Then we have

M=\begin{bmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\ldots\\
d_{0}&d_{1}&d_{2}&d_{3}&\ldots\\
b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&\ldots\\
a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&\ldots\\
0&0&f_{0}&f_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&d_{0}&d_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&b_{0}&b_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&a_{0}&b_{1}&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\\
\end{bmatrix},\quad M^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{0}&f_{1}&f_{2}&f_{3}&\ldots\\
d_{0}&d_{1}&d_{2}&d_{3}&\ldots\\
b_{0}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&\ldots\\
a_{0}-cb_{0}&a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}&\ldots\\
0&0&f_{0}&f_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&d_{0}&d_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&b_{0}&b_{1}&\ldots\\
0&0&a_{0}-cb_{0}&a_{1}-cb_{1}&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Suppose

\Delta=M^{\prime}[\{4,6,8,12\},\{2,3,4,5\}]=\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&2&3&4&5\\
4&a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}&a_{3}-cb_{3}&a_{4}-cb_{4}\\
6&0&d_{0}&d_{1}&d_{2}\\
8&0&a_{0}-cb_{0}&a_{1}-cb_{1}&a_{2}-cb_{2}\\
12&0&0&0&a_{0}-cb_{0}\\
\end{array}.

Then
A(\Delta)=\{4,8,12\}
and
C(\Delta)=\{1,3,5\}
, so
